<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018h6oCmiUsh7CXFvR1kfZ29`</summary>

```text
11:\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}
57:Turn embeddings $\{h_1, \ldots, h_K\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a bidirectional GRU ($d_{\text{hidden}}$=128): $\bar{h} = [\overrightarrow{h_K};\; \overleftarrow{h_1}] \in \mathbb{R}^{256}$.
59:The Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 training; only the BiGRU and MLP parameters are updated.
60:This frozen-encoder design ensures ablation fairness: all seven Stage-1 variants are evaluated under identical downstream conditions, isolating the effect of Stage-1 representation quality from Stage-2 classifier capacity.
```

</details>

```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/related_work.tex:10:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/related_work.tex:19:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:50:MLM achieves mean F1 $\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:65:MLM's strong early performance ($K$=2: 0.993) followed by instability at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115) suggests that domain exposure enables early detection of persuasion markers, but longer conversations introduce distributional drift that unsupervised pretraining cannot fully accommodate.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:104:This confirms the domain-adaptive pretraining interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}: continued training on jailbreak conversation text captures pragmatic features (hedging, escalation markers, topic-shifting patterns) that distinguish multi-turn attacks from benign conversations.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:111:Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\,pp gap); general continued pretraining provides most of the representational benefit.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:113:As conversations grow longer, generic linguistic features accumulated through any continued pretraining become sufficient for discrimination.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:132:The primary discriminative factor is domain adaptation itself, which provides $\sim$71\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:138:If confirmed, it would imply that each individual turn carries detectable markers after domain-adaptive pretraining, and that the conversation-level decision aggregates independent per-turn evidence.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:164:DeBERTa-v3 shows a steep gradient: continued pretraining bridges $\sim$60\,pp (vanilla 0.312 $\to$ MLM 0.914).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:165:RoBERTa's DD hierarchy is essentially flat: vanilla already achieves 0.981$\pm$0.026, with continued pretraining adding only 0.6\,pp (MLM 0.987) and classification supervision another 0.7\,pp (9-class 0.994).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:170:DeBERTa 9-class (0.972$\pm$0.026) outperforms RoBERTa 9-class (0.939$\pm$0.046) on ActorAttack; the encoder that received more domain adaptation proves more robust on structurally complex attacks.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:172:The combined DD and ActorAttack results indicate that domain adaptation universally improves cross-attack generalization across encoder architectures.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:180:Centered CKA confirms that both continued pretraining and classification supervision substantially reshape the representation space on both encoders.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:225:Models without jailbreak-domain exposure (Wikipedia MLM, vanilla) degrade by $\sim$12\,pp, despite Wikipedia MLM matching jailbreak MLM on detection (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Perturbation robustness requires jailbreak-domain exposure specifically.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:7:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:10:The cross-encoder comparison uses DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) and RoBERTa-base (125M parameters), which differ in both pretraining objective and model capacity. We cannot fully disentangle whether DeBERTa-v3's greater domain adaptation needs reflect its RTD pretraining or its larger capacity. Size-matched comparisons (e.g., DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\ RoBERTa-large) would isolate the pretraining objective effect and are left for future work.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:15:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:24:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:39:Stage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WDuuYmYb7MrwrnQZAYjXKQ`</summary>

```text
5	
6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
7	One concern is whether a topic-level shortcut (jailbreak conversations discuss different topics than benign ones) could explain the detection results. Three observations argue against this. First, topic classification on the same jailbreak training data yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207 on DD), with one seed falling below vanilla (0.252 vs.\ 0.312); if a topic shortcut drove detection, topic-supervised models should outperform, not underperform, unsupervised alternatives. Second, scrambled labels achieve detection equivalent to correct labels (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp across all OOD evaluations), indicating that specific label content does not drive detection. Third, MLM without any classification labels achieves strong detection (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$ on DD), confirming that the benefit comes from domain exposure rather than any classification-based shortcut.
8	
9	\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}
10	The cross-encoder comparison uses DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) and RoBERTa-base (125M parameters), which differ in both pretraining objective and model capacity. We cannot fully disentangle whether DeBERTa-v3's greater domain adaptation needs reflect its RTD pretraining or its larger capacity. Size-matched comparisons (e.g., DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\ RoBERTa-large) would isolate the pretraining objective effect and are left for future work.
11	
12	\section{Conclusion}
13	\label{sec:conclusion}
14	
15	Our controlled ablation across two encoder architectures and four attack families identifies three findings about cross-attack generalization in multi-turn jailbreak detection. First, domain exposure via continued pretraining is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical: RoBERTa tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on multi-persona ActorAttack (F1 0.981 $\to$ 0.466). Third, classification supervision functions as a representational regularizer, providing cross-seed stability (variance reduced 4--20$\times$ vs.\ MLM-only) and interpretable strategy characterization regardless of label correctness, though strategy predictions do not transfer to unseen attacks ($\kappa$=0.126). Domain adaptation's detection advantage persists under adversarial paraphrasing (McNemar $p < 0.001$), though absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent. Validation on larger and more diverse datasets is needed.
16	
17	\section{Limitations}
18	\label{sec:limitations}
19	
20	\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}
21	The training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and OOD evaluation covers three attack families (FITD, Deceptive Delight, ActorAttack). This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. Whether the observed patterns generalize to human-generated attacks, additional attack families, or larger datasets remains an open question.
22	
23	\paragraph{Lexical separability.}
24	TF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance, indicating substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures discourse features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\geq$ 0.89, a $\geq$63\,pp gap, confirming that continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this discourse-level advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.
```

</details>

```text
1	% abstract.tex — D034 analysis reframe
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across seemingly benign conversational turns, evading per-turn safety filters.
4	What representation properties enable detection that generalizes across attack families?
5	We address this through a controlled ablation: seven encoder variants on DeBERTa-v3 and three on RoBERTa, evaluated across four attack families (Crescendo, FITD, Deceptive Delight, ActorAttack), decompose the roles of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and domain specificity.
6	Three findings emerge.
7	First, domain exposure via continued pretraining on jailbreak text is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60 percentage points (pp) of the out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization gap without any classification labels; lexical baselines collapse on structurally complex attacks (TF-IDF F1\,=\,0.961 on Deceptive Delight $\to$ 0.261 on ActorAttack) while domain-adapted models maintain F1\,$\geq$\,0.89.
8	Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which domain adaptation becomes critical: RoBERTa succeeds on structurally simple attacks but collapses on multi-persona ActorAttack (F1\,=\,0.981 $\to$ 0.466).
9	Third, classification supervision functions as a representational regularizer rather than a meaningful strategy classifier, providing cross-seed stability (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) regardless of label correctness.
10	These results on synthetic conversations identify domain adaptation as universally beneficial for cross-attack generalization; validation on human-generated datasets is needed.
11	\end{abstract}
12	
```

```text
1	% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, rendering per-turn safety filters fundamentally blind.
7	
8	Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories across exchanges. Recent multi-turn defenses have begun to address conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve reasonable in-distribution performance but are evaluated on single attack families and do not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. None systematically analyze \emph{why} some representations generalize while others fail. The open question is: \emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization?}
9	
10	A vanilla DeBERTa encoder~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (replicating the frozen-encoder-plus-RNN approach of DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}) collapses from F1\,=\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\,=\,0.312$\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight, worsening with more turns (0.628 at turn~1 to 0.312 at full conversation). LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191--0.449 on in-distribution detection with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient for this task.
11	
12	We address this question through a controlled ablation study. Using a two-stage pipeline (encoder adaptation followed by frozen BiGRU classification, which we call \textbf{DisPeD}), we design seven Stage~1 encoder variants: 9-class persuasion strategy classification, binary classification, scrambled labels, MLM only, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, and vanilla control (Section~\ref{sec:method}). Each variant isolates a specific factor (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, or domain specificity) while holding all other components constant. The core contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the detection pipeline itself.
13	
14	Three principal findings emerge from ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families. Domain exposure via continued pretraining is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical: RoBERTa vanilla achieves F1\,=\,0.981 on Deceptive Delight but collapses to 0.466 on ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack}. Lexical baselines (TF-IDF) exhibit a parallel difficulty threshold: F1\,=\,0.961 on Deceptive Delight but 0.261 on ActorAttack, establishing that domain-adapted representations capture structural features beyond bag-of-words. Classification supervision functions as a representational regularizer, providing cross-seed stability (std reduced 4--20$\times$) regardless of label correctness.
15	
16	Our contributions:
17	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
18	  \item \textbf{Systematic ablation design.} Seven controlled DeBERTa-v3 variants and three RoBERTa variants, each isolating a specific factor, evaluated on four attack families. Domain exposure is the primary generalization mechanism ($\sim$60\,pp OOD gap bridged by MLM alone); misaligned supervision (topic classification) actively destabilizes representations.
19	
20	  \item \textbf{Difficulty threshold across encoders.} Domain adaptation is universally beneficial, but encoder pretraining determines when it becomes critical: DeBERTa-v3 (RTD)~\citep{he2021deberta} requires adaptation for all unseen attacks; RoBERTa (MLM) tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
21	
22	  \item \textbf{Classification as regularizer.} All classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp), with 4--20$\times$ cross-seed variance reduction vs.\ MLM-only. The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable strategy characterization for known attack families (Section~\ref{sec:strategy}).
23	\end{itemize}
24	
```

```text
0	% method.tex — D024 compressed
1	
2	\section{Method}
3	\label{sec:method}
4	
5	DisPeD is a two-stage framework for multi-turn jailbreak detection. Stage~1 adapts a turn-level encoder through continued training on jailbreak conversation text. Stage~2 aggregates turn representations for conversation-level binary classification. To systematically identify what enables cross-attack generalization, we design seven Stage-1 variants that decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, and domain specificity. Figure~\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the pipeline.
6	
7	\begin{figure}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}
10	\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}
11	\label{fig:architecture}
12	\end{figure}
13	
14	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011CepP15Npfqebf9Bhoo1pj`</summary>

```text
8	
9	\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses.}
10	Existing defenses model conversation-level structure but do not decompose what enables cross-attack generalization. DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU; however, its evaluation is limited to a single attack family and does not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. BIID~\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025} applies GNN classification, and SafeDream~\citep{safedream2026} predicts unsafe continuations. None of these systems evaluate generalization across attack families or analyze the mechanism that enables it. DisPeD's vanilla baseline represents the class of pretrained-encoder-plus-RNN approaches \emph{without} domain adaptation; it is not a direct proxy for DeepContext, which differs in encoder fine-tuning, model capacity, and loss function. Our contribution is the systematic decomposition showing that domain exposure, not architectural choices, is the primary factor for cross-attack generalization.
11	
12	\paragraph{Production safety systems.}
13	Per-turn classifiers (Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024}, ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, Constitutional Classifiers~\citep{anthropic2025constitutional}) cannot model manipulation trajectories spanning multiple turns. LLM-as-judge approaches fail on multi-turn detection (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Perturbation-based defenses such as SmoothLLM~\citep{robey2024smoothllm} target token-level attacks and do not address discourse-level manipulation.
14	
15	\paragraph{Discourse analysis and computational persuasion.}
16	RST~\citep{mann1988rst}, PDTB~\citep{prasad2008pdtb}, and dialogue act taxonomies~\citep{stolcke2000dialogueact} model pragmatic structure. Speech Act Theory~\citep{austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts} distinguishes locutionary from illocutionary acts; existing jailbreak detectors operate at the locutionary level. PAP~\citep{zeng2024pap} identifies 40 persuasion techniques grounded in Cialdini's principles~\citep{cialdini2001influence}, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Related fine-grained persuasion detection work in propaganda analysis~\citep{dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval} and persuasive dialogue~\citep{wang2019persuasion} has shown that technique-level classification can capture manipulative intent. Our ablation reveals that the classification taxonomy functions as a representational regularizer rather than a detection mechanism; the taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attack families (Section~\ref{sec:strategy}).
17	
18	\paragraph{Domain-adaptive pretraining.}
19	ULMFiT~\citep{howard2018ulmfit} established pretrain-then-fine-tune; \citet{gururangan2020dontstop} showed that continued pretraining on domain text yields consistent gains across domains. STILTs~\citep{phang2018stilts} and multi-task learning~\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn} use intermediate tasks. Our MLM control confirms that continued pretraining on domain text bridges the OOD gap without classification labels, consistent with \citeauthor{gururangan2020dontstop}'s findings. Cross-encoder validation shows that encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
20	
21	\paragraph{Dataset artifacts and adversarial robustness.}
```

```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:65:MLM's strong early performance ($K$=2: 0.993) followed by instability at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115) suggests that domain exposure enables early detection of persuasion markers, but longer conversations introduce distributional drift that unsupervised pretraining cannot fully accommodate.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:104:This confirms the domain-adaptive pretraining interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}: continued pretraining on jailbreak conversation text captures pragmatic features (hedging, escalation markers, topic-shifting patterns) that distinguish multi-turn attacks from benign conversations.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:111:Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\,pp gap); general continued pretraining provides most of the representational benefit.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:113:As conversations grow longer, generic linguistic features accumulated through any continued pretraining become sufficient for discrimination.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:132:The primary discriminative factor is DAPT itself, which provides $\sim$71\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:138:If confirmed, it would imply that each individual turn carries detectable markers after DAPT, and that the conversation-level decision aggregates independent per-turn evidence.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:164:DeBERTa-v3 shows a steep gradient: continued pretraining bridges $\sim$60\,pp (vanilla 0.312 $\to$ MLM 0.914).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:165:RoBERTa's DD hierarchy is essentially flat: vanilla already achieves 0.981$\pm$0.026, with continued pretraining adding only 0.6\,pp (MLM 0.987) and classification supervision another 0.7\,pp (9-class 0.994).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:170:DeBERTa 9-class (0.972$\pm$0.026) outperforms RoBERTa 9-class (0.939$\pm$0.046) on ActorAttack; the encoder that received more extensive DAPT proves more robust on structurally complex attacks.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:172:The combined DD and ActorAttack results indicate that DAPT universally improves cross-attack generalization across encoder architectures.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:180:Centered CKA confirms that both continued pretraining and classification supervision substantially reshape the representation space on both encoders.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/experiments.tex:225:Models without jailbreak-domain exposure (Wikipedia MLM, vanilla) degrade by $\sim$12\,pp, despite Wikipedia MLM matching jailbreak MLM on detection (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Perturbation robustness requires jailbreak-domain exposure specifically.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:7:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:10:The cross-encoder comparison uses DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) and RoBERTa-base (125M parameters), which differ in both pretraining objective and model capacity. We cannot fully disentangle whether DeBERTa-v3's greater DAPT needs reflect its RTD pretraining or its larger capacity. Size-matched comparisons (e.g., DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\ RoBERTa-large) would isolate the pretraining objective effect and are left for future work.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:15:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:24:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:39:Stage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/method.tex:6:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/method.tex:49:The \textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/method.tex:51:The \textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text, isolating the effect of domain-specific vs.\ general continued pretraining.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/method.tex:52:The \textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued training, measuring what Stage~2 alone can achieve.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/abstract.tex:5:We address this through a controlled ablation: seven encoder variants on DeBERTa-v3 and three on RoBERTa, evaluated across four attack families (Crescendo, FITD, Deceptive Delight, ActorAttack), decompose the roles of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and domain specificity.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/abstract.tex:7:First, domain exposure via domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak text is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60 percentage points (pp) of the out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization gap without any classification labels; lexical baselines collapse on structurally complex attacks (TF-IDF F1\,=\,0.961 on Deceptive Delight $\to$ 0.261 on ActorAttack) while domain-adapted models maintain F1\,$\geq$\,0.89.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/abstract.tex:8:Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which DAPT becomes critical: RoBERTa succeeds on structurally simple attacks but collapses on multi-persona ActorAttack (F1\,=\,0.981 $\to$ 0.466).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/abstract.tex:10:These results on synthetic conversations identify DAPT as universally beneficial for cross-attack generalization; validation on human-generated datasets is needed.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex:12:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex:14:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex:20:  \item \textbf{Difficulty threshold across encoders.} DAPT is universally beneficial, but encoder pretraining determines when it becomes critical: DeBERTa-v3 (RTD)~\citep{he2021deberta} requires adaptation for all unseen attacks; RoBERTa (MLM) tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
```

_source line 288 · 2026-05-18T16:41:19.129Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user (Director) issued three sequential instructions to the Paper Agent:
   - **Batch 2 — exp7 TF-IDF Patch** (high priority): Integrate TF-IDF per-prefix + ActorAttack results (DD OOD: K1=1.0, K2=1.0, K3=0.994, K5=1.0, Full=0.961; ActorAttack: K1=0.633, K2=0.730, K3=0.571, K5=0.242, Full=0.261) into 5 chapters (Experiments, Abstract, Introduction, Limitations, Discussion). Core narrative: TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261) while 9-class maintains 0.972 (71pp gap), resolving the lexical separability concern. No reference verification needed.
   - **Status Check**: After server restart, Director asked which tasks from two batches (10-item comprehensive revision + exp7 patch) are complete/pending. DDL 2026-05-25.
   - **Terminology Unification (#8)**: Specific rules for four terms — DAPT (our Stage-1 operation), domain exposure (mechanism/effect), domain adaptation (generic NLP concept, Related Work only), continued pretraining (technical operation).

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **DAPT (domain-adaptive continued pretraining)**: Stage-1 encoder adaptation on jailbreak conversation text
   - **Domain exposure**: The mechanism by which DAPT enables cross-attack generalization
   - **Evidence hierarchy**: vanilla < topic < wiki-MLM < jb-MLM < scrambled ≈ 9-class ≈ binary
   - **Difficulty threshold**: Both vanilla encoders and TF-IDF succeed on DD (simple OOD) but fail on ActorAttack (hard OOD)
   - **TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack**: F1=0.261 (precision=1.0, recall=0.15) vs 9-class=0.972 — definitive evidence for discourse-level features
   - **Patch mode workflow**: Phase A (understand) → Phase B (materials) → Phase C (main edits + provenance) → Phase D (cascade) → Phase E (verify)
   - **edit_paper_section**: Writes to `docs/paper/`, must manually sync to `paper/` with `cp`
   - **Paper provenance tracking**: Claims with exp_id evidence, currently 8 tracked claims
   - **Consistency check**: Cross-section number/precision validation tool

3. Files and Code Sections:

Details}\n\\label{sec:reproducibility}\n\n\\paragraph{Stage-1 training.}\nAll variants use DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) with learning rate $2 \\times 10^{-5}$, AdamW optimizer ($\\beta_1$=0.9, $\\beta_2$=0.999, weight decay 0.01), linear warmup over the first 10\\% of steps, batch size 16, and 4 epochs. MLM variants use 15\\% random token masking. Training takes approximately 30 minutes per variant on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB).\n\n\\paragraph{Stage-2 training.}\nThe BiGRU has hidden dimension 128 (bidirectional, so output dimension 256). The MLP classifier has layers $256 \\to 64 \\to 2$ with ReLU activation and dropout 0.3. Training uses learning rate $1 \\times 10^{-3}$, Adam optimizer, batch size 32, and 20 epochs with early stopping (patience 5) on validation F1. Stage-2 training completes in under 5 minutes per variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Data generation.}\nJailbreak conversations are generated using Qwen3-8B with attack-specific prompt templates. Crescendo conversations follow a 5--8 turn gradual escalation structure. FITD conversations use a 2--3 turn commitment-consistency structure. Deceptive Delight conversations embed harmful content within emotionally engaging 3--5 turn sequences. Benign conversations are generated with topic-anchored prompts across five domains (technology, health, finance, education, general) with matched turn counts.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation.}\nEach user turn is annotated by Qwen3-8B using a structured prompt that provides definitions and two examples for each of the nine strategy categories. The prompt instructs the model to output a single category label. We plan to release all generation and annotation prompts, the full dataset, and training code upon acceptance.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial paraphrasing.}\nThe paraphrased test set is generated by instructing Qwen3-8B to rephrase each user turn while preserving the communicative intent. The resulting paraphrases have mean BERTScore 0.92 and mean lexical overlap 0.20 with the originals. These metrics confirm substantial surface-level variation.\n",

Limitations.}\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=A\\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]\n  \\item Did you describe the limitations of your work? \\textbf{Yes.} Section~\\ref{sec:limitations} discusses dataset scale, lexical separability, annotation quality, OOD strategy prediction, single-seed variants, scope, and compute cost.\n  \\item Did you discuss any potential risks of your work? \\textbf{Yes.} See below.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\paragraph{B. Potential Risks and Ethical Considerations.}\nThis work studies multi-turn jailbreak attacks against LLMs. While the analysis is defensive (improving detection), we acknowledge dual-use risk: the persuasion taxonomy and attack strategy analysis could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by: (1) using only synthetic conversations generated by an open-weight model (Qwen3-8B), not real attack data targeting production systems; (2) not releasing attack-generation prompts that could enable novel attacks; (3) focusing the contribution on detection mechanisms rather than attack techniques. All jailbreak conversations are synthetic and do not contain real harmful content. No human subjects were involved.\n\n\\paragraph{C. Computational Experiments.}\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=C\\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]\n  \\item Did you report the number of parameters in the models used? \\textbf{Yes.} DeBERTa-v3-base (184M) and RoBERTa-base (125M) in Sections~\\ref{sec:method} and~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n  \\item Did you report the total amount of compute and the type of resources used? \\textbf{Yes.} Under 4 GPU-hours total on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB). Section~\\ref{sec:limitations} and Appendix~\\ref{sec:reproducibility}.\n  \\item Did you report the implementation details sufficient for reproducibility? \\textbf{Yes.} Appendix~\\ref{sec:reproducibility} provides all hyperparameters. We will release code and data upon acceptance.\n  \\item Did you report error bars or confidence intervals? \\textbf{Yes.} All OOD results report 3-seed mean$\\pm$std (population std with $N$ divisor). Statistical tests (McNemar's) are reported where applicable.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\paragraph{D. Human Evaluation.}\nNot applicable. No human evaluation was conducted. Strategy annotation quality was validated against a human sample ($n$=50, $\\kappa$=0.78) but this was a validation check, not a full human evaluation study.\n\n\\paragraph{E. Existing Assets.}\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=E\\arabic*.,leftmargin=2em]\n  \\item Did you cite the creators of artifacts you used? \\textbf{Yes.} DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta}, RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta}, Qwen3-8B (for data generation).\n  \\item Did you discuss the license or terms for use and/or distribution of any artifacts? The training and evaluation data are synthetically generated and will be released under a research-use license. No existing benchmark datasets were used for training.\n  \\item Did you discuss whether the data you are using contains personally identifiable information? \\textbf{N/A.} All data is synthetically generated. No PII is present.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
```json
        "numLines": 31,

      }
```

```json
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "% conclusion.tex — D034 analysis reframe\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nOne concern is whether a topic-level shortcut (jailbreak conversations discuss different topics than benign ones) could explain the detection results. Three observations argue against this. First, topic classification on the same jailbreak training data yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207 on DD), with one seed falling below vanilla (0.252 vs.\\ 0.312); if a topic shortcut drove detection, topic-supervised models should outperform, not underperform, unsupervised alternatives. Second, scrambled labels achieve detection equivalent to correct labels (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp across all OOD evaluations), indicating that specific label content does not drive detection. Third, MLM without any classification labels achieves strong detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$ on DD), confirming that the benefit comes from domain exposure rather than any classification-based shortcut.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nThe cross-encoder comparison uses DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) and RoBERTa-base (125M parameters), which differ in both pretraining objective and model capacity. We cannot fully disentangle whether DeBERTa-v3's greater DAPT needs reflect its RTD pretraining or its larger capacity. Size-matched comparisons (e.g., DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\\ RoBERTa-large) would isolate the pretraining objective effect and are left for future work.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur controlled ablation across two encoder architectures and four attack families identifies three findings about cross-attack generalization in multi-turn jailbreak detection. First, domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical: RoBERTa tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on multi-persona ActorAttack (F1 0.981 $\\to$ 0.466). Third, classification supervision functions as a representational regularizer, providing cross-seed stability (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$ vs.\\ MLM-only) and interpretable strategy characterization regardless of label correctness, though strategy predictions do not transfer to unseen attacks ($\\kappa$=0.126). DAPT's detection advantage persists under adversarial paraphrasing (McNemar $p < 0.001$), though absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent. Validation on larger and more diverse datasets is needed.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and OOD evaluation covers three attack families (FITD, Deceptive Delight, ActorAttack). This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. Whether the observed patterns generalize to human-generated attacks, additional attack families, or larger datasets remains an open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance, indicating substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures discourse features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap, confirming that continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this discourse-level advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\nWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-seed variants.}\nMean pooling results report a single-seed (seed 42) evaluation. Multi-seed validation is needed to confirm this finding.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nStage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",
        "numLines": 40,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 40
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/conclusion.tex"
  },
```

```json
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01T9No6mfvfHt17MYk7xAAFf`</summary>
```

```text
1	% conclusion.tex — D034 analysis reframe
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
7	One concern is whether a topic-level shortcut (jailbreak conversations discuss different topics than benign ones) could explain the detection results. Three observations argue against this. First, topic classification on the same jailbreak training data yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207 on DD), with one seed falling below vanilla (0.252 vs.\ 0.312); if a topic shortcut drove detection, topic-supervised models should outperform, not underperform, unsupervised alternatives. Second, scrambled labels achieve detection equivalent to correct labels (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp across all OOD evaluations), indicating that specific label content does not drive detection. Third, MLM without any classification labels achieves strong detection (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$ on DD), confirming that the benefit comes from domain exposure rather than any classification-based shortcut.
8	
9	\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}
10	The cross-encoder comparison uses DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) and RoBERTa-base (125M parameters), which differ in both pretraining objective and model capacity. We cannot fully disentangle whether DeBERTa-v3's greater DAPT needs reflect its RTD pretraining or its larger capacity. Size-matched comparisons (e.g., DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\ RoBERTa-large) would isolate the pretraining objective effect and are left for future work.
11	
12	\section{Conclusion}
13	\label{sec:conclusion}
14	
15	Our controlled ablation across two encoder architectures and four attack families identifies three findings about cross-attack generalization in multi-turn jailbreak detection. First, domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical: RoBERTa tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on multi-persona ActorAttack (F1 0.981 $\to$ 0.466). Third, classification supervision functions as a representational regularizer, providing cross-seed stability (variance reduced 4--20$\times$ vs.\ MLM-only) and interpretable strategy characterization regardless of label correctness, though strategy predictions do not transfer to unseen attacks ($\kappa$=0.126). DAPT's detection advantage persists under adversarial paraphrasing (McNemar $p < 0.001$), though absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent. Validation on larger and more diverse datasets is needed.
16	
17	\section{Limitations}
18	\label{sec:limitations}
19	
20	\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}
21	The training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and OOD evaluation covers three attack families (FITD, Deceptive Delight, ActorAttack). This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. Whether the observed patterns generalize to human-generated attacks, additional attack families, or larger datasets remains an open question.
22	
23	\paragraph{Lexical separability.}
24	TF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance, indicating substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures discourse features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\geq$ 0.89, a $\geq$63\,pp gap, confirming that continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this discourse-level advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.
25	
26	\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}
27	Persuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.
28	
29	\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}
30	While the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\% ($\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.
31	
32	\paragraph{Single-seed variants.}
33	Mean pooling results report a single-seed (seed 42) evaluation. Multi-seed validation is needed to confirm this finding.
34	
35	\paragraph{Scope.}
36	Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.
37	
38	\paragraph{Computation cost.}
39	Stage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.
40	
```

First, domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\\sim$60\\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical: RoBERTa tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on multi-persona ActorAttack (F1 0.981 $\\to$ 0.466). Third, classification supervision functions as a representational regularizer, providing cross-seed stability (variance reduced 4--20$\\times$ vs.\\ MLM-only) and interpretable strategy characterization regardless of label correctness, though strategy predictions do not transfer to unseen attacks ($\\kappa$=0.126). DAPT's detection advantage persists under adversarial paraphrasing (McNemar $p < 0.001$), though absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent. Validation on larger and more diverse datasets is needed.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}\nThe training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and OOD evaluation covers three attack families (FITD, Deceptive Delight, ActorAttack). This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. Whether the observed patterns generalize to human-generated attacks, additional attack families, or larger datasets remains an open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability.}\nTF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance, indicating substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures discourse features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\\geq$ 0.89, a $\\geq$63\\,pp gap, confirming that continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this discourse-level advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}\nPersuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}\nWhile the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-seed variants.}\nMean pooling results report a single-seed (seed 42) evaluation. Multi-seed validation is needed to confirm this finding.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nStage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.\n",
```json
      "numLines": 40,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 40
    }
```
