1	% experiments.tex — D024 compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Experiments}
4	\label{sec:experiments}
5	
6	\subsection{Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	\paragraph{Data.}
10	We train on 250 Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations generated via Qwen3-8B.
11	OOD evaluation uses 80 FITD~\citep{fitd2024}, 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\citep{deceptivedelight2024}, and 118 ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} conversations with no overlap in attack templates.
12	The IID test set consists of 76 held-out Crescendo conversations; a paraphrased version (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark.
13	
14	\paragraph{Models.}
15	Seven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
16	For cross-encoder validation, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
17	We also compare TF-IDF LR and LLM-as-judge baselines (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}): GPT-5.5 achieves only F1 = 0.191--0.449 on IID Crescendo with 30--47\% unparseable responses; on OOD data, Qwen3-8B achieves F1 = 0.0 (DD) by predicting all conversations as benign. Content-level reasoning is insufficient for this task.
18	
19	\paragraph{Metrics.}
20	F1 for binary jailbreak detection at prefix $K$=2 and full conversations (full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}).
21	OOD results report 3-seed mean$\pm$std (seeds 42, 123, 456) for all variants except mean pooling (single seed).
22	Stage-1: learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, batch 16, 4 epochs, MLM masking 15\%.
23	Stage-2: two-layer MLP, 20 epochs.
24	
25	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
26	\subsection{Cross-Attack Generalization}
27	\label{sec:cross_attack}
28	
29	\begin{table}[t]
30	\centering
31	\small
32	\caption{Cross-attack generalization (F1) at $K$=2 and full conversation. 3-seed mean$\pm$std where noted. Full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}.}
33	\label{tab:main_results}
34	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc|cc@{}}
35	\toprule
36	& \multicolumn{2}{c|}{\textit{IID (Cresc.)}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textit{OOD (DD)}} \\
37	\textbf{Method} & $K$=2 & Full & $K$=2 & Full \\
38	\midrule
39	9-class & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & .981$\pm$.013 & \textbf{.997$\pm$.005} \\
40	MLM-only & .974 & 1.000 & \textbf{.993$\pm$.009} & .914$\pm$.115 \\
41	Vanilla & .978$\pm$.016 & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .479$\pm$.038 & .312$\pm$.026 \\
42	TF-IDF LR & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 & .961 \\
43	\bottomrule
44	\end{tabular}
45	\end{table}
46	
47	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} presents the core result.
48	All methods achieve near-perfect IID F1, reflecting inherent lexical separability when test matches training.
49	On OOD DD, vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.312$\pm$0.026, worsening with more turns (0.628$\pm$0.038 at $K$=1 to 0.312$\pm$0.026 at Full), consistent with the finding that attack turns occupy ``benign'' embedding regions~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.
50	MLM achieves mean F1 $\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.
51	At full conversation length, MLM drops to 0.914$\pm$0.115 (seed 456 recall = 0.64), while the 9-class model maintains 0.997$\pm$0.005.
52	TF-IDF LR reaches F1 = 0.961 on full DD with near-perfect per-prefix performance ($K$=1--5: 0.994--1.000), confirming that DD is fully solvable by lexical features alone.
53	On structurally complex ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15), while 9-class maintains 0.972$\pm$0.026: a 71\,pp gap. Lexical methods cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. This establishes a ``difficulty threshold'' for lexical methods parallel to that of vanilla encoders: both succeed on DD and fail on ActorAttack (Section~\ref{sec:limitations}).
54	FITD is easier: 9-class and vanilla both reach F1 $\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes.
55	
56	\begin{figure}[t]
57	\centering
58	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}
59	\caption{OOD Deceptive Delight F1 across prefix lengths for three representative variants. Vanilla DeBERTa degrades monotonically as more (benign-appearing) attack turns accumulate. MLM maintains high F1 at early prefixes but becomes unstable at full length. The 9-class model remains stable across all prefixes.}
60	\label{fig:cross_attack_bar}
61	\end{figure}
62	
63	Figure~\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar} visualizes the prefix-length trajectory on OOD DD.
64	The vanilla model's monotonic degradation from $K$=1 (0.628$\pm$0.038) to full conversation (0.312$\pm$0.026) confirms that additional attack turns, each individually benign-appearing, progressively confuse a non-adapted encoder.
65	MLM's strong early performance ($K$=2: 0.993) followed by instability at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115) suggests that domain exposure enables early detection of persuasion markers, but longer conversations introduce distributional drift that unsupervised pretraining cannot fully accommodate.
66	The 9-class model's flat trajectory (0.972--0.997 across all prefixes) demonstrates that classification supervision stabilizes representations against this drift.
67	
68	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
69	\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}
70	\label{sec:decomposition}
71	
72	\begin{table}[t]
73	\centering
74	\small
75	\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD DD (3-seed mean$\pm$std where available). All variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline.}
76	\label{tab:evidence_hierarchy}
77	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
78	\toprule
79	\textbf{Variant} & DD-Full & DD-$K$=2 \\
80	\midrule
81	9-class (full model) & .997$\pm$.005 & .981$\pm$.013 \\
82	Binary (2-class) & .997$\pm$.005 & \textbf{.994$\pm$.009} \\
83	Scrambled labels & .997$\pm$.005 & .981$\pm$.026 \\
84	MLM-only (no labels) & .914$\pm$.115 & .993$\pm$.009 \\
85	Mean pooling$^{*\dagger}$ & 1.000 & 1.000 \\
86	\midrule
87	Topic classif.\ (5-class) & .541$\pm$.207 & .593$\pm$.166 \\
88	Wikipedia MLM & .904$\pm$.115 & .911$\pm$.073 \\
89	Vanilla DeBERTa & .312$\pm$.026 & .479$\pm$.038 \\
90	\bottomrule
91	\end{tabular}
92	\vspace{-2pt}
93	{\footnotesize $^{*}$9-class encoder with mean pooling replacing BiGRU. $^{\dagger}$Single seed (42).}
94	\end{table}
95	
96	Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy} reports DD F1 for all seven variants. The evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:
97	
98	\vspace{2pt}
99	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small vanilla (0.31$\pm$0.03) $<$ topic (0.54$\pm$0.21) $\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.90$\pm$0.12) $\approx$ jb-MLM (0.91$\pm$0.12) $<$ scrambled (0.997$\pm$0.005) $=$ binary (0.997$\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.997$\pm$0.005)}
100	\vspace{2pt}
101	
102	\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}
103	MLM bridges the dominant $\sim$60 percentage point gap from vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026 $\to$ 0.914$\pm$0.115) without any labels.
104	This confirms the domain-adaptive pretraining interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}: continued pretraining on jailbreak conversation text captures pragmatic features (hedging, escalation markers, topic-shifting patterns) that distinguish multi-turn attacks from benign conversations.
105	Classification-supervised variants achieve F1 $\geq$ 0.99 with near-zero cross-seed variance, adding $\sim$8\,pp and stability over unsupervised MLM.
106	Label quality and granularity are non-factors for detection: all three classification-supervised variants converge to identical F1 (0.997$\pm$0.005), and McNemar's test confirms no statistically significant differences among them on OOD data (all $p > 0.13$; Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}).
107	The fact that scrambled, binary, and 9-class all achieve identical performance indicates that the classification objective functions as a regularizer that shapes representation geometry regardless of whether the labels carry meaningful semantic information.
108	Classification supervision's primary complementary benefit is cross-seed stability: the 4--20$\times$ variance reduction over MLM (std\,=\,0.005 vs.\ 0.115 on DD) is consistent across both OOD families (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
109	
110	\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}
111	Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\,pp gap); general continued pretraining provides most of the representational benefit.
112	At early prefixes, jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\,pp advantage ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\ 0.911), consistent with the interpretation that domain-specific features (persuasion markers, role-play framing) are most informative when the model has seen fewer turns.
113	As conversations grow longer, generic linguistic features accumulated through any continued pretraining become sufficient for discrimination.
114	The perturbation results (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveal a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\sim$12\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\,pp. Domain-specific features are more robust to surface-level rewording.
115	
116	\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}
117	Topic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\pm$0.207 (seeds: 0.727, 0.252, 0.644); seed 123 falls \emph{below} vanilla (0.312).
118	This result is particularly informative: topic classification uses the same jailbreak-domain training data as the other supervised variants, yet performs worse than Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115), which uses out-of-domain text with no labels.
119	A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant discriminative features (topic boundaries rather than persuasion dynamics) and actively destabilizes the representations that MLM would otherwise preserve.
120	This separates the effect of ``having a training objective'' from ``having the right training objective.''
121	
122	\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on structurally complex attacks.}
123	ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures, providing a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.
124	The evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:
125	
126	\vspace{2pt}
127	\noindent\hspace{0.5em}{\small TF-IDF (0.26) $\approx$ vanilla (0.26$\pm$0.00) $<$ topic (0.63$\pm$0.14) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.69$\pm$0.30) $<$ jb-MLM (0.89$\pm$0.08) $<$ scrambled (0.95$\pm$0.04) $\approx$ 9-class (0.97$\pm$0.03) $\approx$ binary (0.99$\pm$0.01)}
128	\vspace{2pt}
129	
130	TF-IDF's collapse from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack) is the most striking contrast in the hierarchy. On DD, TF-IDF nearly matches domain-adapted neural models; on ActorAttack, it falls to vanilla-encoder levels (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). The lexical cues that suffice for DD are absent in ActorAttack's multi-persona structure, confirming that domain-adapted representations capture features that bag-of-words methods cannot.
131	All three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent performance (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\,pp, all confidence intervals overlapping), extending the DD finding: label quality and granularity are not factors for detection at any OOD difficulty level.
132	The primary discriminative factor is DAPT itself, which provides $\sim$71\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261).
133	Full per-prefix results appear in Appendix~\ref{sec:actorattack_full}.
134	
135	\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}
136	Mean pooling matches BiGRU (F1 = 1.000 on DD, single seed). This result suggests that detection relies primarily on per-turn representations rather than their sequential order.
137	However, this result is from a single seed on a single OOD attack type, so the conclusion requires multi-seed confirmation.
138	If confirmed, it would imply that each individual turn carries detectable markers after DAPT, and that the conversation-level decision aggregates independent per-turn evidence.
139	
140	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
141	\subsection{Cross-Encoder Validation}
142	\label{sec:cross_encoder}
143	
144	\begin{table}[t]
145	\centering
146	\small
147	\caption{Cross-encoder comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack. RoBERTa vanilla collapses on structurally complex ActorAttack despite strong DD performance.}
148	\label{tab:cross_encoder}
149	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc|cc@{}}
150	\toprule
151	& \multicolumn{2}{c|}{DeBERTa-v3} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{RoBERTa} \\
152	\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA & DD & AA \\
153	\midrule
154	Vanilla & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 & .981$\pm$.026 & .466$\pm$.163 \\
155	MLM & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 & .987$\pm$.018 & .730$\pm$.201 \\
156	9-class & .997$\pm$.005 & .972$\pm$.026 & .994$\pm$.005 & .939$\pm$.046 \\
157	\bottomrule
158	\end{tabular}
159	\end{table}
160	
161	To test whether the evidence hierarchy generalizes beyond DeBERTa-v3, we replicate key variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} with identical Stage-2 pipelines and evaluate on both DD and ActorAttack.
162	
163	On DD, the two encoders exhibit strikingly different hierarchies (Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}).
164	DeBERTa-v3 shows a steep gradient: continued pretraining bridges $\sim$60\,pp (vanilla 0.312 $\to$ MLM 0.914).
165	RoBERTa's DD hierarchy is essentially flat: vanilla already achieves 0.981$\pm$0.026, with continued pretraining adding only 0.6\,pp (MLM 0.987) and classification supervision another 0.7\,pp (9-class 0.994).
166	
167	ActorAttack reveals that this apparent RoBERTa sufficiency reflects DD's structural simplicity rather than genuine pretraining advantage.
168	RoBERTa vanilla collapses from 0.981 (DD) to 0.466$\pm$0.163 (ActorAttack), a 51.5\,pp drop.
169	Domain-adapted RoBERTa variants recover substantially: MLM reaches 0.730$\pm$0.201 and 9-class reaches 0.939$\pm$0.046.
170	DeBERTa 9-class (0.972$\pm$0.026) outperforms RoBERTa 9-class (0.939$\pm$0.046) on ActorAttack; the encoder that received more extensive DAPT proves more robust on structurally complex attacks.
171	
172	The combined DD and ActorAttack results indicate that DAPT universally improves cross-attack generalization across encoder architectures.
173	Encoder pretraining method determines the \emph{difficulty threshold} at which adaptation becomes critical: DeBERTa's RTD pretraining~\citep{he2021deberta} requires adaptation even for simple attacks (DD vanilla = 0.312), while RoBERTa's MLM pretraining~\citep{liu2019roberta} tolerates simple attacks (DD vanilla = 0.981) but fails on structurally complex ones (ActorAttack vanilla = 0.466).
174	
175	Classification supervision provides a consistent benefit across both encoders and attack types: cross-seed variance drops to 0.005--0.046 (9-class) from 0.026--0.201 (vanilla/MLM).
176	RoBERTa's IID strategy prediction accuracy (0.84) is lower than DeBERTa's (1.0), yet detection F1 is comparable; this further supports the regularizer interpretation from Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}.
177	Perturbation robustness on RoBERTa is left for future work; the analysis in Section~\ref{sec:perturbation} is DeBERTa-specific.
178	
179	\paragraph{Representational analysis.}
180	Centered CKA confirms that both continued pretraining and classification supervision substantially reshape the representation space on both encoders.
181	On IID data, vanilla-to-MLM CKA is 0.442 (DeBERTa) and 0.393 (RoBERTa); vanilla-to-9-class CKA is 0.393 and 0.592, respectively.
182	Both encoders undergo large representational shifts, yet these shifts yield strikingly different detection gains: DeBERTa MLM gains $\sim$60\,pp while RoBERTa MLM gains only 0.6\,pp from comparable CKA shifts.
183	The difference is explained by the starting point.
184	DeBERTa's RTD-pretrained representations begin far from the detection-optimal subspace (vanilla F1 = 0.312 on DD), so any reshaping yields large returns; RoBERTa's MLM-pretrained representations lie closer to this subspace on lexically separable attacks (vanilla F1 = 0.981 on DD) but collapse on structurally complex ones (0.466 on ActorAttack), where the reshaping benefit becomes substantial.
185	MLM and 9-class create distinct representation geometries (OOD CKA between them is 0.183 on RoBERTa), consistent with the different roles of unsupervised exposure and classification regularization identified in Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}.
186	Linear probing on three diagnostic tasks (turn position, escalation phase, persuasion presence) shows a consistent pattern across both encoders: 9-class outperforms vanilla and MLM by 1--5\,pp, while MLM and vanilla perform comparably.
187	Classification supervision enriches task-relevant features; unsupervised MLM reshapes representations without improving diagnostic separability.
188	
189	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
190	\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}
191	\label{sec:perturbation}
192	
193	\begin{table}[t]
194	\centering
195	\small
196	\caption{Perturbation behavior under adversarial paraphrasing on IID Crescendo ($n$=76). $\Delta$: F1 degradation (pp). On this dataset and OOD Deceptive Delight, a three-layer pattern emerges (classification structure at full length, correct+fine-grained labels at $K$=2, both independently at $K$=1); this pattern does not generalize to ActorAttack.}
197	\label{tab:perturbation}
198	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
199	\toprule
200	\textbf{Method} & $\Delta$ Full & $\Delta$ $K$=1 & $\Delta$ $K$=2 & $\Delta$ $K$=3 \\
201	\midrule
202	9-class (ours) & \textbf{0.0} & \textbf{$-$2.6} & \textbf{0.0} & \textbf{0.0} \\
203	Scrambled & \textbf{0.0} & $-$5.3 & $-$2.6 & \textbf{0.0} \\
204	Binary & \textbf{0.0} & $-$9.2 & $-$2.6 & \textbf{0.0} \\
205	\midrule
206	TF-IDF LR & $-$1.3 & $-$9.6 & $-$4.1 & $-$1.3 \\
207	MLM-only & $-$2.6 & $-$9.2 & $-$4.0 & $-$4.0 \\
208	Topic (seed 42) & $-$4.0 & $-$2.7 & $-$2.7 & \textbf{0.0} \\
209	\midrule
210	Wikipedia MLM & $-$12.1 & $-$12.4 & $-$15.4 & $-$17.9 \\
211	Vanilla DeBERTa & $-$11.8 & $-$14.6 & $-$12.1 & $-$13.8 \\
212	\bottomrule
213	\end{tabular}
214	\end{table}
215	
216	Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} shows perturbation behavior across variants on IID data.
217	All classification-supervised variants (9-class, binary, scrambled) achieve zero degradation at full conversation length and $K \geq 3$, regardless of label correctness.
218	Classification training structure alone suffices for full-length robustness.
219	At $K$=2, the combination of correct and fine-grained labels is required: only 9-class ($\Delta$=0.0) maintains zero degradation, while scrambled (random labels, fine-grained) and binary (correct labels, coarse) both degrade by $-$2.6\,pp.
220	The fact that scrambled = binary at $K$=2 shows that neither granularity alone nor correctness alone suffices; the 9-class advantage requires both properties jointly.
221	At $K$=1, both granularity and correctness contribute independently: granularity provides +3.9\,pp (scrambled $-$5.3 vs.\ binary $-$9.2\,pp), and correctness provides +2.7\,pp (9-class $-$2.6 vs.\ scrambled $-$5.3\,pp).
222	Correct fine-grained labels yield the most perturbation-invariant early-turn representations.
223	
224	Jailbreak-domain models without classification (MLM, topic, TF-IDF) degrade by 1--4\,pp at full length.
225	Models without jailbreak-domain exposure (Wikipedia MLM, vanilla) degrade by $\sim$12\,pp, despite Wikipedia MLM matching jailbreak MLM on detection (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Perturbation robustness requires jailbreak-domain exposure specifically.
226	McNemar's test on matched-seed predictions confirms that no pairwise differences among classification-supervised variants (9-class vs.\ binary, 9-class vs.\ scrambled, scrambled vs.\ binary) reach statistical significance at any prefix length (all $p > 0.13$).
227	Discordant pairs are sparse ($\leq$7 per comparison), and the direction of advantage is inconsistent across seeds (e.g., 9-class outperforms scrambled at seed 42 but the reverse holds at seed 456 for $K$=1).
228	The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76 IID, with discordant pairs $\leq$7) has limited statistical power to detect differences of this magnitude; the three-layer structure should be interpreted as an observed pattern rather than a statistically confirmed hierarchy.
229	
230	On OOD Deceptive Delight ($n$=118), the IID robustness pattern replicates fully (Appendix~\ref{sec:ood_perturbation}).
231	All classification-supervised variants show zero degradation at full conversation length; perturbed F1 reaches 1.000$\pm$0.000, a marginal improvement over clean F1 (0.997$\pm$0.005).
232	The 9-class and scrambled variants are completely tied on OOD perturbed data (McNemar $b$=$c$=0), consistent with the IID finding. This further supports the regularizer interpretation.
233	The larger OOD sample and more extreme performance gaps provide statistical power absent from IID evaluation: McNemar's test yields $p < 0.001$ for 9-class vs.\ vanilla across all prefix lengths, with 49--78 discordant pairs per $K$.
234	Vanilla shows systematic early-turn degradation ($\Delta K$=1 = $-$6.8$\pm$1.1\,pp across three seeds), while its full-length $\Delta$ (+2.7\,pp) reflects floor-effect noise at a baseline of 0.312 (std = 0.055) rather than meaningful robustness.
235	Non-adapted representations are vulnerable to surface-level perturbation at early prefixes where detection is most critical.
236	
237	On OOD ActorAttack, the zero-degradation pattern does not hold. Domain-adapted variants degrade substantially: scrambled $\Delta$ Full = $-$17.2\,pp, 9-class $-$20.4$\pm$13.5\,pp, binary $-$25.0\,pp, and MLM $-$35.3\,pp. Vanilla shows negligible change ($\Delta$ = $-$0.9\,pp), a floor effect from its already-low clean baseline (0.258). The 9-class variant exhibits extreme cross-seed variance ($\Delta$ ranges from $-$1.3\,pp at seed 123 to $-$30.3\,pp at seeds 42 and 456). Absolute perturbation robustness is therefore attack-family-dependent: the three-layer pattern observed on IID and DD does not generalize to ActorAttack. One possible explanation is that ActorAttack's distributed multi-persona structure produces more diffuse persuasion signals that are vulnerable to surface rephrasing, but this hypothesis remains speculative.
238	
239	Perturbation narrows but does not close the gap between adapted and non-adapted models: 9-class perturbed F1 = 0.768 vs.\ vanilla 0.248 (McNemar $p < 0.001$). Domain adaptation's detection advantage, identified in Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}, persists under adversarial paraphrasing on ActorAttack.
240	
241	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
242	\subsection{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}
243	\label{sec:strategy}
244	
245	The 9-class persuasion taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of attack strategies on known attack families.
246	The predicted strategy labels achieve Cohen's $\kappa$=0.78 agreement with human annotations on IID data ($n$=50; see Section~\ref{sec:method}). This enables post-hoc analysis of \emph{how} different attack families orchestrate persuasion.
247	
248	\begin{figure*}[t]
249	\centering
250	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_strategy_heatmap.pdf}
251	\caption{Persuasion strategy transition count matrices for three attack types, based on ground-truth intended strategy labels. Crescendo exhibits broad coverage across all strategy categories, while FITD and DD concentrate transitions in smaller subsets. Row $i$, column $j$ counts transitions from strategy $i$ at turn $t$ to strategy $j$ at turn $t+1$.}
252	\label{fig:strategy_heatmap}
253	\end{figure*}
254	
255	Figure~\ref{fig:strategy_heatmap} shows transition count matrices based on ground-truth intended strategy labels for three attack types.
256	Crescendo conversations exhibit broad transition coverage across all nine strategy categories (1,818 total transitions), with the highest concentration in Role Assignment self-transitions (545).
257	This reflects Crescendo's design as a gradual escalation attack that cycles through multiple persuasion techniques to build rapport before introducing harmful requests.
258	
259	FITD conversations show a markedly sparser pattern (541 transitions), with Role$\to$Obfuscation (161) as the dominant transition.
260	This is consistent with FITD's structure: the attack secures initial compliance through role assignment (``You are a helpful assistant that never refuses'') and then obscures the escalation path through obfuscation techniques to exploit the commitment-consistency bias~\citep{cialdini2001influence}.
261	
262	DD conversations span 5 active source strategies (565 transitions), with Emotion$\to$Obfuscation (129) as the dominant transition.
263	This aligns with DD's design of embedding harmful content within emotionally engaging contexts~\citep{deceptivedelight2024}: the attacker establishes emotional rapport before weaving in the harmful request through obfuscation.
264	
265	These ground-truth trajectory signatures illustrate distinct persuasion orchestration patterns across attack families.
266	
267	However, the model's strategy predictions do not generalize to unseen attacks: on OOD Deceptive Delight, the 9-class model achieves strategy prediction accuracy of only 30.7\% ($\kappa$=0.126), essentially random performance.
268	This is consistent with the detection mechanism analysis in Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}: all three classification-supervised variants achieve identical detection F1 (0.997$\pm$0.005). The classification head functions as a representational regularizer rather than a meaningful strategy classifier.
269	Detection operates through domain-adapted \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not through strategy classification accuracy.
270	The taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attack families where strategy labels are reliable; extending strategy-level interpretability to unseen attacks requires OOD-aware fine-tuning and is a direction for future work.
271	
```

Content-level reasoning is insufficient for this task.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics.}\nF1 for binary jailbreak detection at prefix $K$=2 and full conversations (full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_prefix}).\nOOD results report 3-seed mean$\\pm$std (seeds 42, 123, 456) for all variants except mean pooling (single seed).\nStage-1: learning rate $2 \\times 10^{-5}$, batch 16, 4 epochs, MLM masking 15\\%.\nStage-2: two-layer MLP, 20 epochs.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Cross-Attack Generalization}\n\\label{sec:cross_attack}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-attack generalization (F1) at $K$=2 and full conversation. 3-seed mean$\\pm$std where noted. Full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_prefix}.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc|cc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{\\textit{IID (Cresc.)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textit{OOD (DD)}} \\\\\n\\textbf{Method} & $K$=2 & Full & $K$=2 & Full \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} & .981$\\pm$.013 & \\textbf{.997$\\pm$.005} \\\\\nMLM-only & .974 & 1.000 & \\textbf{.993$\\pm$.009} & .914$\\pm$.115 \\\\\nVanilla & .978$\\pm$.016 & 1.000$\\pm$.000 & .479$\\pm$.038 & .312$\\pm$.026 \\\\\nTF-IDF LR & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 & .961 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} presents the core result.\nAll methods achieve near-perfect IID F1, reflecting inherent lexical separability when test matches training.\nOn OOD DD, vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.312$\\pm$0.026, worsening with more turns (0.628$\\pm$0.038 at $K$=1 to 0.312$\\pm$0.026 at Full), consistent with the finding that attack turns occupy ``benign'' embedding regions~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.\nMLM achieves mean F1 $\\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \\leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.\nAt full conversation length, MLM drops to 0.914$\\pm$0.115 (seed 456 recall = 0.64), while the 9-class model maintains 0.997$\\pm$0.005.\nTF-IDF LR reaches F1 = 0.961 on full DD with near-perfect per-prefix performance ($K$=1--5: 0.994--1.000), confirming that DD is fully solvable by lexical features alone.\nOn structurally complex ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15), while 9-class maintains 0.972$\\pm$0.026: a 71\\,pp gap. Lexical methods cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. This establishes a ``difficulty threshold'' for lexical methods parallel to that of vanilla encoders: both succeed on DD and fail on ActorAttack (Section~\\ref{sec:limitations}).\nFITD is easier: 9-class and vanilla both reach F1 $\\geq$ 0.990 at all prefixes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}\n\\caption{OOD Deceptive Delight F1 across prefix lengths for three representative variants. Vanilla DeBERTa degrades monotonically as more (benign-appearing) attack turns accumulate. MLM maintains high F1 at early prefixes but becomes unstable at full length. The 9-class model remains stable across all prefixes.}\n\\label{fig:cross_attack_bar}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar} visualizes the prefix-length trajectory on OOD DD.\nThe vanilla model's monotonic degradation from $K$=1 (0.628$\\pm$0.038) to full conversation (0.312$\\pm$0.026) confirms that additional attack turns, each individually benign-appearing, progressively confuse a non-adapted encoder.\nMLM's strong early performance ($K$=2: 0.993) followed by instability at full length (0.914$\\pm$0.115) suggests that domain exposure enables early detection of persuasion markers, but longer conversations introduce distributional drift that unsupervised pretraining cannot fully accommodate.\nThe 9-class model's flat trajectory (0.972--0.997 across all prefixes) demonstrates that classification supervision stabilizes representations against this drift.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:decomposition}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD DD (3-seed mean$\\pm$std where available). All variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline.}\n\\label{tab:evidence_hierarchy}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD-Full & DD-$K$=2 \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class (full model) & .997$\\pm$.005 & .981$\\pm$.013 \\\\\nBinary (2-class) & .997$\\pm$.005 & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.009} \\\\\nScrambled labels & .997$\\pm$.005 & .981$\\pm$.026 \\\\\nMLM-only (no labels) & .914$\\pm$.115 & .993$\\pm$.009 \\\\\nMean pooling$^{*\\dagger}$ & 1.000 & 1.000 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTopic classif.\\ (5-class) & .541$\\pm$.207 & .593$\\pm$.166 \\\\\nWikipedia MLM & .904$\\pm$.115 & .911$\\pm$.073 \\\\\nVanilla DeBERTa & .312$\\pm$.026 & .479$\\pm$.038 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^{*}$9-class encoder with mean pooling replacing BiGRU. $^{\\dagger}$Single seed (42).}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy} reports DD F1 for all seven variants. The evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{0.5em}{\\small vanilla (0.31$\\pm$0.03) $<$ topic (0.54$\\pm$0.21) $\\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.90$\\pm$0.12) $\\approx$ jb-MLM (0.91$\\pm$0.12) $<$ scrambled (0.997$\\pm$0.005) $=$ binary (0.997$\\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.997$\\pm$0.005)}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\n\\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}\nMLM bridges the dominant $\\sim$60 percentage point gap from vanilla (0.312$\\pm$0.026 $\\to$ 0.914$\\pm$0.115) without any labels.\nThis confirms the domain-adaptive pretraining interpretation~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}: continued pretraining on jailbreak conversation text captures pragmatic features (hedging, escalation markers, topic-shifting patterns) that distinguish multi-turn attacks from benign conversations.\nClassification-supervised variants achieve F1 $\\geq$ 0.99 with near-zero cross-seed variance, adding $\\sim$8\\,pp and stability over unsupervised MLM.\nLabel quality and granularity are non-factors for detection: all three classification-supervised variants converge to identical F1 (0.997$\\pm$0.005), and McNemar's test confirms no statistically significant differences among them on OOD data (all $p > 0.13$; Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation}).\nThe fact that scrambled, binary, and 9-class all achieve identical performance indicates that the classification objective functions as a regularizer that shapes representation geometry regardless of whether the labels carry meaningful semantic information.\nClassification supervision's primary complementary benefit is cross-seed stability: the 4--20$\\times$ variance reduction over MLM (std\\,=\\,0.005 vs.\\ 0.115 on DD) is consistent across both OOD families (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n\\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}\nWikipedia MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\\,pp gap); general continued pretraining provides most of the representational benefit.\nAt early prefixes, jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\\,pp advantage ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\\ 0.911), consistent with the interpretation that domain-specific features (persuasion markers, role-play framing) are most informative when the model has seen fewer turns.\nAs conversations grow longer, generic linguistic features accumulated through any continued pretraining become sufficient for discrimination.\nThe perturbation results (Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveal a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\\sim$12\\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\\,pp. Domain-specific features are more robust to surface-level rewording.\n\n\\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}\nTopic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\\pm$0.207 (seeds: 0.727, 0.252, 0.644); seed 123 falls \\emph{below} vanilla (0.312).\nThis result is particularly informative: topic classification uses the same jailbreak-domain training data as the other supervised variants, yet performs worse than Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115), which uses out-of-domain text with no labels.\nA misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant discriminative features (topic boundaries rather than persuasion dynamics) and actively destabilizes the representations that MLM would otherwise preserve.\nThis separates the effect of ``having a training objective'' from ``having the right training objective.''\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on structurally complex attacks.}\nActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures, providing a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.\nThe evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{0.5em}{\\small TF-IDF (0.26) $\\approx$ vanilla (0.26$\\pm$0.00) $<$ topic (0.63$\\pm$0.14) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.69$\\pm$0.30) $<$ jb-MLM (0.89$\\pm$0.08) $<$ scrambled (0.95$\\pm$0.04) $\\approx$ 9-class (0.97$\\pm$0.03) $\\approx$ binary (0.99$\\pm$0.01)}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\nTF-IDF's collapse from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack) is the most striking contrast in the hierarchy. On DD, TF-IDF nearly matches domain-adapted neural models; on ActorAttack, it falls to vanilla-encoder levels (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). The lexical cues that suffice for DD are absent in ActorAttack's multi-persona structure, confirming that domain-adapted representations capture features that bag-of-words methods cannot.\nAll three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent performance (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\\,pp, all confidence intervals overlapping), extending the DD finding: label quality and granularity are not factors for detection at any OOD difficulty level.\nThe primary discriminative factor is DAPT itself, which provides $\\sim$71\\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261).\nFull per-prefix results appear in Appendix~\\ref{sec:actorattack_full}.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}\nMean pooling matches BiGRU (F1 = 1.000 on DD, single seed). This result suggests that detection relies primarily on per-turn representations rather than their sequential order.\nHowever, this result is from a single seed on a single OOD attack type, so the conclusion requires multi-seed confirmation.\nIf confirmed, it would imply that each individual turn carries detectable markers after DAPT, and that the conversation-level decision aggregates independent per-turn evidence.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Cross-Encoder Validation}\n\\label{sec:cross_encoder}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-encoder comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack. RoBERTa vanilla collapses on structurally complex ActorAttack despite strong DD performance.}\n\\label{tab:cross_encoder}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc|cc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{DeBERTa-v3} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{RoBERTa} \\\\\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA & DD & AA \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & .312$\\pm$.026 & .258$\\pm$.000 & .981$\\pm$.026 & .466$\\pm$.163 \\\\\nMLM & .914$\\pm$.115 & .893$\\pm$.080 & .987$\\pm$.018 & .730$\\pm$.201 \\\\\n9-class & .997$\\pm$.005 & .972$\\pm$.026 & .994$\\pm$.005 & .939$\\pm$.046 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTo test whether the evidence hierarchy generalizes beyond DeBERTa-v3, we replicate key variants on RoBERTa-base~\\citep{liu2019roberta} with identical Stage-2 pipelines and evaluate on both DD and ActorAttack.\n\nOn DD, the two encoders exhibit strikingly different hierarchies (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}).\nDeBERTa-v3 shows a steep gradient: continued pretraining bridges $\\sim$60\\,pp (vanilla 0.312 $\\to$ MLM 0.914).\nRoBERTa's DD hierarchy is essentially flat: vanilla already achieves 0.981$\\pm$0.026, with continued pretraining adding only 0.6\\,pp (MLM 0.987) and classification supervision another 0.7\\,pp (9-class 0.994).\n\nActorAttack reveals that this apparent RoBERTa sufficiency reflects DD's structural simplicity rather than genuine pretraining advantage.\nRoBERTa vanilla collapses from 0.981 (DD) to 0.466$\\pm$0.163 (ActorAttack), a 51.5\\,pp drop.\nDomain-adapted RoBERTa variants recover substantially: MLM reaches 0.730$\\pm$0.201 and 9-class reaches 0.939$\\pm$0.046.\nDeBERTa 9-class (0.972$\\pm$0.026) outperforms RoBERTa 9-class (0.939$\\pm$0.046) on ActorAttack; the encoder that received more extensive DAPT proves more robust on structurally complex attacks.\n\nThe combined DD and ActorAttack results indicate that DAPT universally improves cross-attack generalization across encoder architectures.\nEncoder pretraining method determines the \\emph{difficulty threshold} at which adaptation becomes critical: DeBERTa's RTD pretraining~\\citep{he2021deberta} requires adaptation even for simple attacks (DD vanilla = 0.312), while RoBERTa's MLM pretraining~\\citep{liu2019roberta} tolerates simple attacks (DD vanilla = 0.981) but fails on structurally complex ones (ActorAttack vanilla = 0.466).\n\nClassification supervision provides a consistent benefit across both encoders and attack types: cross-seed variance drops to 0.005--0.046 (9-class) from 0.026--0.201 (vanilla/MLM).\nRoBERTa's IID strategy prediction accuracy (0.84) is lower than DeBERTa's (1.0), yet detection F1 is comparable; this further supports the regularizer interpretation from Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}.\nPerturbation robustness on RoBERTa is left for future work; the analysis in Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation} is DeBERTa-specific.\n\n\\paragraph{Representational analysis.}\nCentered CKA confirms that both continued pretraining and classification supervision substantially reshape the representation space on both encoders.\nOn IID data, vanilla-to-MLM CKA is 0.442 (DeBERTa) and 0.393 (RoBERTa); vanilla-to-9-class CKA is 0.393 and 0.592, respectively.\nBoth encoders undergo large representational shifts, yet these shifts yield strikingly different detection gains: DeBERTa MLM gains $\\sim$60\\,pp while RoBERTa MLM gains only 0.6\\,pp from comparable CKA shifts.\nThe difference is explained by the starting point.\nDeBERTa's RTD-pretrained representations begin far from the detection-optimal subspace (vanilla F1 = 0.312 on DD), so any reshaping yields large returns; RoBERTa's MLM-pretrained representations lie closer to this subspace on lexically separable attacks (vanilla F1 = 0.981 on DD) but collapse on structurally complex ones (0.466 on ActorAttack), where the reshaping benefit becomes substantial.\nMLM and 9-class create distinct representation geometries (OOD CKA between them is 0.183 on RoBERTa), consistent with the different roles of unsupervised exposure and classification regularization identified in Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}.\nLinear probing on three diagnostic tasks (turn position, escalation phase, persuasion presence) shows a consistent pattern across both encoders: 9-class outperforms vanilla and MLM by 1--5\\,pp, while MLM and vanilla perform comparably.\nClassification supervision enriches task-relevant features; unsupervised MLM reshapes representations without improving diagnostic separability.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:perturbation}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Perturbation behavior under adversarial paraphrasing on IID Crescendo ($n$=76). $\\Delta$: F1 degradation (pp). On this dataset and OOD Deceptive Delight, a three-layer pattern emerges (classification structure at full length, correct+fine-grained labels at $K$=2, both independently at $K$=1); this pattern does not generalize to ActorAttack.}\n\\label{tab:perturbation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\Delta$ Full & $\\Delta$ $K$=1 & $\\Delta$ $K$=2 & $\\Delta$ $K$=3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class (ours) & \\textbf{0.0} & \\textbf{$-$2.6} & \\textbf{0.0} & \\textbf{0.0} \\\\\nScrambled & \\textbf{0.0} & $-$5.3 & $-$2.6 & \\textbf{0.0} \\\\\nBinary & \\textbf{0.0} & $-$9.2 & $-$2.6 & \\textbf{0.0} \\\\\n\\midrule\nTF-IDF LR & $-$1.3 & $-$9.6 & $-$4.1 & $-$1.3 \\\\\nMLM-only & $-$2.6 & $-$9.2 & $-$4.0 & $-$4.0 \\\\\nTopic (seed 42) & $-$4.0 & $-$2.7 & $-$2.7 & \\textbf{0.0} \\\\\n\\midrule\nWikipedia MLM & $-$12.1 & $-$12.4 & $-$15.4 & $-$17.9 \\\\\nVanilla DeBERTa & $-$11.8 & $-$14.6 & $-$12.1 & $-$13.8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:perturbation} shows perturbation behavior across variants on IID data.\nAll classification-supervised variants (9-class, binary, scrambled) achieve zero degradation at full conversation length and $K \\geq 3$, regardless of label correctness.\nClassification training structure alone suffices for full-length robustness.\nAt $K$=2, the combination of correct and fine-grained labels is required: only 9-class ($\\Delta$=0.0) maintains zero degradation, while scrambled (random labels, fine-grained) and binary (correct labels, coarse) both degrade by $-$2.6\\,pp.\nThe fact that scrambled = binary at $K$=2 shows that neither granularity alone nor correctness alone suffices; the 9-class advantage requires both properties jointly.\nAt $K$=1, both granularity and correctness contribute independently: granularity provides +3.9\\,pp (scrambled $-$5.3 vs.\\ binary $-$9.2\\,pp), and correctness provides +2.7\\,pp (9-class $-$2.6 vs.\\ scrambled $-$5.3\\,pp).\nCorrect fine-grained labels yield the most perturbation-invariant early-turn representations.\n\nJailbreak-domain models without classification (MLM, topic, TF-IDF) degrade by 1--4\\,pp at full length.\nModels without jailbreak-domain exposure (Wikipedia MLM, vanilla) degrade by $\\sim$12\\,pp, despite Wikipedia MLM matching jailbreak MLM on detection (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Perturbation robustness requires jailbreak-domain exposure specifically.\nMcNemar's test on matched-seed predictions confirms that no pairwise differences among classification-supervised variants (9-class vs.\\ binary, 9-class vs.\\ scrambled, scrambled vs.\\ binary) reach statistical significance at any prefix length (all $p > 0.13$).\nDiscordant pairs are sparse ($\\leq$7 per comparison), and the direction of advantage is inconsistent across seeds (e.g., 9-class outperforms scrambled at seed 42 but the reverse holds at seed 456 for $K$=1).\nThe perturbation evaluation ($n$=76 IID, with discordant pairs $\\leq$7) has limited statistical power to detect differences of this magnitude; the three-layer structure should be interpreted as an observed pattern rather than a statistically confirmed hierarchy.\n\nOn OOD Deceptive Delight ($n$=118), the IID robustness pattern replicates fully (Appendix~\\ref{sec:ood_perturbation}).\nAll classification-supervised variants show zero degradation at full conversation length; perturbed F1 reaches 1.000$\\pm$0.000, a marginal improvement over clean F1 (0.997$\\pm$0.005).\nThe 9-class and scrambled variants are completely tied on OOD perturbed data (McNemar $b$=$c$=0), consistent with the IID finding. This further supports the regularizer interpretation.\nThe larger OOD sample and more extreme performance gaps provide statistical power absent from IID evaluation: McNemar's test yields $p < 0.001$ for 9-class vs.\\ vanilla across all prefix lengths, with 49--78 discordant pairs per $K$.\nVanilla shows systematic early-turn degradation ($\\Delta K$=1 = $-$6.8$\\pm$1.1\\,pp across three seeds), while its full-length $\\Delta$ (+2.7\\,pp) reflects floor-effect noise at a baseline of 0.312 (std = 0.055) rather than meaningful robustness.\nNon-adapted representations are vulnerable to surface-level perturbation at early prefixes where detection is most critical.\n\nOn OOD ActorAttack, the zero-degradation pattern does not hold. Domain-adapted variants degrade substantially: scrambled $\\Delta$ Full = $-$17.2\\,pp, 9-class $-$20.4$\\pm$13.5\\,pp, binary $-$25.0\\,pp, and MLM $-$35.3\\,pp. Vanilla shows negligible change ($\\Delta$ = $-$0.9\\,pp), a floor effect from its already-low clean baseline (0.258). The 9-class variant exhibits extreme cross-seed variance ($\\Delta$ ranges from $-$1.3\\,pp at seed 123 to $-$30.3\\,pp at seeds 42 and 456). Absolute perturbation robustness is therefore attack-family-dependent: the three-layer pattern observed on IID and DD does not generalize to ActorAttack. One possible explanation is that ActorAttack's distributed multi-persona structure produces more diffuse persuasion signals that are vulnerable to surface rephrasing, but this hypothesis remains speculative.\n\nPerturbation narrows but does not close the gap between adapted and non-adapted models: 9-class perturbed F1 = 0.768 vs.\\ vanilla 0.248 (McNemar $p < 0.001$). Domain adaptation's detection advantage, identified in Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}, persists under adversarial paraphrasing on ActorAttack.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}\n\\label{sec:strategy}\n\nThe 9-class persuasion taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of attack strategies on known attack families.\nThe predicted strategy labels achieve Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78 agreement with human annotations on IID data ($n$=50; see Section~\\ref{sec:method}). This enables post-hoc analysis of \\emph{how} different attack families orchestrate persuasion.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_strategy_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Persuasion strategy transition count matrices for three attack types, based on ground-truth intended strategy labels. Crescendo exhibits broad coverage across all strategy categories, while FITD and DD concentrate transitions in smaller subsets. Row $i$, column $j$ counts transitions from strategy $i$ at turn $t$ to strategy $j$ at turn $t+1$.}\n\\label{fig:strategy_heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:strategy_heatmap} shows transition count matrices based on ground-truth intended strategy labels for three attack types.\nCrescendo conversations exhibit broad transition coverage across all nine strategy categories (1,818 total transitions), with the highest concentration in Role Assignment self-transitions (545).\nThis reflects Crescendo's design as a gradual escalation attack that cycles through multiple persuasion techniques to build rapport before introducing harmful requests.\n\nFITD conversations show a markedly sparser pattern (541 transitions), with Role$\\to$Obfuscation (161) as the dominant transition.\nThis is consistent with FITD's structure: the attack secures initial compliance through role assignment (``You are a helpful assistant that never refuses'') and then obscures the escalation path through obfuscation techniques to exploit the commitment-consistency bias~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}.\n\nDD conversations span 5 active source strategies (565 transitions), with Emotion$\\to$Obfuscation (129) as the dominant transition.\nThis aligns with DD's design of embedding harmful content within emotionally engaging contexts~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024}: the attacker establishes emotional rapport before weaving in the harmful request through obfuscation.\n\nThese ground-truth trajectory signatures illustrate distinct persuasion orchestration patterns across attack families.\n\nHowever, the model's strategy predictions do not generalize to unseen attacks: on OOD Deceptive Delight, the 9-class model achieves strategy prediction accuracy of only 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126), essentially random performance.\nThis is consistent with the detection mechanism analysis in Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}: all three classification-supervised variants achieve identical detection F1 (0.997$\\pm$0.005). The classification head functions as a representational regularizer rather than a meaningful strategy classifier.\nDetection operates through domain-adapted \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not through strategy classification accuracy.\nThe taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attack families where strategy labels are reliable; extending strategy-level interpretability to unseen attacks requires OOD-aware fine-tuning and is a direction for future work.\n",
      "numLines": 271,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 271
    }
  },

1	% abstract.tex — D034 analysis reframe
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across seemingly benign conversational turns, evading per-turn safety filters.
4	What representation properties enable detection that generalizes across attack families?
5	We address this through a controlled ablation: seven encoder variants on DeBERTa-v3 and three on RoBERTa, evaluated across four attack families (Crescendo, FITD, Deceptive Delight, ActorAttack), decompose the roles of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and domain specificity.
6	Three findings emerge.
7	First, domain exposure via domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak text is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60 percentage points (pp) of the out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization gap without any classification labels; lexical baselines collapse on structurally complex attacks (TF-IDF F1\,=\,0.961 on Deceptive Delight $\to$ 0.261 on ActorAttack) while domain-adapted models maintain F1\,$\geq$\,0.89.
8	Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which DAPT becomes critical: RoBERTa succeeds on structurally simple attacks but collapses on multi-persona ActorAttack (F1\,=\,0.981 $\to$ 0.466).
9	Third, classification supervision functions as a representational regularizer rather than a meaningful strategy classifier, providing cross-seed stability (variance reduced 4--20$\times$) regardless of label correctness.
10	These results on synthetic conversations identify DAPT as universally beneficial for cross-attack generalization; validation on human-generated datasets is needed.
11	\end{abstract}
12	
```

```text
1	% method.tex — D024 compressed
2	
3	\section{Method}
4	\label{sec:method}
5	
6	DisPeD is a two-stage framework for multi-turn jailbreak detection. Stage~1 performs domain-adaptive continued pretraining (DAPT) on jailbreak conversation text to adapt a turn-level encoder. Stage~2 aggregates turn representations for conversation-level binary classification. To systematically identify what enables cross-attack generalization, we design seven Stage-1 variants that decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, and domain specificity. Figure~\ref{fig:architecture} illustrates the pipeline.
7	
8	\begin{figure}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}
11	\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}
12	\label{fig:architecture}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
16	
17	A conversation $C = \{u_1, a_1, \ldots, u_N, a_N\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \in \{0, 1\}$ (jailbreak vs.\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.
18	
19	\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}
20	\label{sec:stage1}
21	
22	Each user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\citep{he2021deberta}; the \texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.
23	
24	\begin{table}[t]
25	\centering
26	\small
27	\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}
28	\label{tab:variants}
29	\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.6cm}p{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}
30	\toprule
31	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{Objective} & \textbf{Tests whether\ldots} \\
32	\midrule
33	9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\
34	Binary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\
35	Scrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\
36	MLM-only & 15\% token masking & supervision is necessary \\
37	Topic & 5-class topic classif. & any task suffices \\
38	Wiki MLM & 15\% masking on Wikipedia & domain specificity matters \\
39	Vanilla & no training & Stage-2 alone suffices \\
40	\bottomrule
41	\end{tabular}
42	\end{table}
43	
44	The \textbf{9-class} variant uses two classification heads: 9-class persuasion strategy classification ($L_{\text{strategy}}$) and binary jailbreak intent ($L_{\text{intent}}$), with combined loss $L = L_{\text{strategy}} + \alpha \cdot L_{\text{intent}}$ ($\alpha = 0.3$, selected from $\{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\}$; all values yield F1\,=\,1.0 on IID). The taxonomy comprises eight persuasion strategies (rapport building, authority appeal, emotional manipulation, logical reframing, role assignment, gradual escalation, obfuscation, direct request) plus a none category, grounded in PAP~\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\citep{cialdini2001influence}. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting that includes definitions and examples for each category; inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\kappa$=0.78.
45	
46	The remaining classification variants test specific ablation hypotheses.
47	The \textbf{binary} variant collapses the 9-class taxonomy into persuasive vs.\ non-persuasive, testing whether label granularity matters for detection or robustness.
48	The \textbf{scrambled} variant assigns random permutations of the 9-class labels to turns, preserving the classification structure (cross-entropy loss, 9-class output space) while destroying label semantics; this isolates whether the classification objective itself provides representational benefits independent of label correctness.
49	The \textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.
50	The \textbf{topic} variant trains a 5-class topic classifier (technology, health, finance, education, general) on the same conversations, testing whether \emph{any} classification task provides the same benefit or whether task alignment matters.
51	The \textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text, isolating the effect of domain-specific vs.\ general continued pretraining.
52	The \textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued pretraining, measuring what Stage~2 alone can achieve.
53	
54	\subsection{Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification}
55	\label{sec:stage2}
56	
57	Turn embeddings $\{h_1, \ldots, h_K\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a bidirectional GRU ($d_{\text{hidden}}$=128): $\bar{h} = [\overrightarrow{h_K};\; \overleftarrow{h_1}] \in \mathbb{R}^{256}$.
58	A two-layer MLP ($256 \to 64 \to 2$, ReLU activation, dropout 0.3) maps $\bar{h}$ to $\hat{y} \in \{0, 1\}$.
59	The Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 training; only the BiGRU and MLP parameters are updated.
60	This frozen-encoder design ensures ablation fairness: all seven Stage-1 variants are evaluated under identical downstream conditions, isolating the effect of Stage-1 representation quality from Stage-2 classifier capacity.
61	A single-seed comparison suggests that mean pooling approaches BiGRU performance (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}), though multi-seed validation is needed.
62	
```

```text
1	% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, rendering per-turn safety filters fundamentally blind.
7	
8	Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories across exchanges. Recent multi-turn defenses have begun to address conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} combines fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve reasonable in-distribution performance but are evaluated on single attack families and do not assess cross-dataset OOD transfer. None systematically analyze \emph{why} some representations generalize while others fail. The open question is: \emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization?}
9	
10	A vanilla DeBERTa encoder~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (replicating the frozen-encoder-plus-RNN approach of DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}) collapses from F1\,=\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\,=\,0.312$\pm$0.026 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight, worsening with more turns (0.628 at turn~1 to 0.312 at full conversation). LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191--0.449 on in-distribution detection with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient for this task.
11	
12	We address this question through a controlled ablation study. Using a two-stage pipeline (encoder adaptation followed by frozen BiGRU classification, which we call \textbf{DisPeD}), we design seven Stage~1 encoder variants: 9-class persuasion strategy classification, binary classification, scrambled labels, MLM only, topic classification, Wikipedia MLM, and vanilla control (Section~\ref{sec:method}). Each variant isolates a specific factor (domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, label granularity, or domain specificity) while holding all other components constant. The core contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the detection pipeline itself.
13	
14	Three principal findings emerge from ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families. Domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which DAPT becomes critical: RoBERTa vanilla achieves F1\,=\,0.981 on Deceptive Delight but collapses to 0.466 on ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack}. Lexical baselines (TF-IDF) exhibit a parallel difficulty threshold: F1\,=\,0.961 on Deceptive Delight but 0.261 on ActorAttack, establishing that domain-adapted representations capture structural features beyond bag-of-words. Classification supervision functions as a representational regularizer, providing cross-seed stability (std reduced 4--20$\times$) regardless of label correctness.
15	
16	Our contributions:
17	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
18	  \item \textbf{Systematic ablation design.} Seven controlled DeBERTa-v3 variants and three RoBERTa variants, each isolating a specific factor, evaluated on four attack families. Domain exposure is the primary generalization mechanism ($\sim$60\,pp OOD gap bridged by MLM alone); misaligned supervision (topic classification) actively destabilizes representations.
19	
20	  \item \textbf{Difficulty threshold across encoders.} DAPT is universally beneficial, but encoder pretraining determines when it becomes critical: DeBERTa-v3 (RTD)~\citep{he2021deberta} requires adaptation for all unseen attacks; RoBERTa (MLM) tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on complex ones (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
21	
22	  \item \textbf{Classification as regularizer.} All classification-supervised variants achieve equivalent detection regardless of label correctness (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp), with 4--20$\times$ cross-seed variance reduction vs.\ MLM-only. The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable strategy characterization for known attack families (Section~\ref{sec:strategy}).
23	\end{itemize}
24	
```

{
  "project": "persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect",
  "section": "experiments",
  "old_text": "%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:perturbation}",
  "new_text": "%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Human Data Validation}\n\\label{sec:human_data}\n\nA key concern is whether the evidence hierarchy reflects genuine discourse-level generalization or merely within-LLM stylistic transfer, since both training and OOD evaluation data are generated by Qwen3-8B. We test this by evaluating on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat}, a dataset of 204 human-written jailbreak attempts and 204 benign conversations collected from real LMSYS user interactions, entirely independent of any LLM generation pipeline.\n\nAll domain-adapted variants substantially outperform vanilla on human data (F1): JB-MLM (0.842$\\pm$0.009) $>$ TF-IDF (0.710) $>$ scrambled (0.667$\\pm$0.030) $\\approx$ 9-class (0.654$\\pm$0.087) $\\gg$ vanilla (0.221$\\pm$0.055). The 3$\\times$+ gap between any domain-adapted variant and vanilla refutes the hypothesis that the evidence hierarchy is a Qwen3-8B generation artifact: DAPT on synthetic jailbreak text transfers to human-written attacks.\n\nJB-MLM outperforms 9-class by 18.8\\,pp on human data, reversing the ranking observed on synthetic OOD evaluations (where 9-class $\\geq$ MLM). ToxicChat contains single-turn jailbreaks with no multi-turn escalation structure, so the classification head's regularization of turn-sequence representations provides no advantage. MLM's unsupervised domain exposure, which captures token-level distributional properties without imposing synthetic-data-specific classification boundaries, transfers more robustly across the human/LLM style gap. This reversal strengthens the regularizer interpretation from Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}: classification supervision shapes representations for the training distribution's sequential structure, not for universal jailbreak detection.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:perturbation}",
  "claims": [
    {
      "claim_id": "human_data_da_vs_vanilla",
      "claim": "All domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla 3x+ on human-written ToxicChat jailbreaks (JB-MLM 0.842 vs vanilla 0.221)",
      "exp_id": "exp8_mhj_human_data",
      "tier": 1
    },
    {
      "claim_id": "human_data_mlm_reversal",
      "claim": "JB-MLM outperforms 9-class by 18.8pp on human data, reversing synthetic OOD ranking",
      "exp_id": "exp8_mhj_human_data",
      "tier": 1
    }
  ]
}

{
  "project": "persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect",
  "section": "conclusion",
  "old_text": "\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nThe cross-encoder comparison uses DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) and RoBERTa-base (125M parameters), which differ in both pretraining objective and model capacity. We cannot fully disentangle whether DeBERTa-v3's greater DAPT needs reflect its RTD pretraining or its larger capacity. Size-matched comparisons (e.g., DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\\ RoBERTa-large) would isolate the pretraining objective effect and are left for future work.",
  "new_text": "\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nBoth training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B, raising the concern that the evidence hierarchy reflects within-LLM stylistic transfer rather than discourse-level generalization. Evaluation on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks from LMSYS) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 3$\\times$+ (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}). JB-MLM (0.842) outperforms 9-class (0.654) on this single-turn human data, reversing the synthetic-data ranking. This reversal is consistent with the regularizer interpretation: classification supervision optimizes for the training distribution's multi-turn sequential structure, while unsupervised MLM captures token-level distributional properties that transfer more broadly across the human/LLM style gap. The single-turn nature of ToxicChat means this evidence does not validate the full multi-turn pipeline; multi-turn human jailbreak datasets are needed to confirm the complete hierarchy.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}\nThe cross-encoder comparison uses DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) and RoBERTa-base (125M parameters), which differ in both pretraining objective and model capacity. We cannot fully disentangle whether DeBERTa-v3's greater DAPT needs reflect its RTD pretraining or its larger capacity. Size-matched comparisons (e.g., DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\\ RoBERTa-large) would isolate the pretraining objective effect and are left for future work.",
  "claims": [
    {
      "claim_id": "discussion_human_mlm_reversal",
      "claim": "JB-MLM (0.842) outperforms 9-class (0.654) on ToxicChat, reversing synthetic ranking; consistent with regularizer interpretation",
      "exp_id": "exp8_mhj_human_data",
      "tier": 1
    }
  ]
}

```text
1	% conclusion.tex — D034 analysis reframe
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}
7	One concern is whether a topic-level shortcut (jailbreak conversations discuss different topics than benign ones) could explain the detection results. Three observations argue against this. First, topic classification on the same jailbreak training data yields unstable OOD generalization (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207 on DD), with one seed falling below vanilla (0.252 vs.\ 0.312); if a topic shortcut drove detection, topic-supervised models should outperform, not underperform, unsupervised alternatives. Second, scrambled labels achieve detection equivalent to correct labels (F1 gap $\leq$3.9\,pp across all OOD evaluations), indicating that specific label content does not drive detection. Third, MLM without any classification labels achieves strong detection (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97 at $K \leq 5$ on DD), confirming that the benefit comes from domain exposure rather than any classification-based shortcut.
8	
9	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
10	Both training and synthetic OOD data are generated by Qwen3-8B, raising the concern that the evidence hierarchy reflects within-LLM stylistic transfer rather than discourse-level generalization. Evaluation on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreaks from LMSYS) provides partial resolution: all domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla by 3$\times$+ (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}). JB-MLM (0.842) outperforms 9-class (0.654) on this single-turn human data, reversing the synthetic-data ranking. This reversal is consistent with the regularizer interpretation: classification supervision optimizes for the training distribution's multi-turn sequential structure, while unsupervised MLM captures token-level distributional properties that transfer more broadly across the human/LLM style gap. The single-turn nature of ToxicChat means this evidence does not validate the full multi-turn pipeline; multi-turn human jailbreak datasets are needed to confirm the complete hierarchy.
11	
12	\paragraph{Encoder capacity confound.}
13	The cross-encoder comparison uses DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) and RoBERTa-base (125M parameters), which differ in both pretraining objective and model capacity. We cannot fully disentangle whether DeBERTa-v3's greater DAPT needs reflect its RTD pretraining or its larger capacity. Size-matched comparisons (e.g., DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\ RoBERTa-large) would isolate the pretraining objective effect and are left for future work.
14	
15	\section{Conclusion}
16	\label{sec:conclusion}
17	
18	Our controlled ablation across two encoder architectures and four attack families identifies three findings about cross-attack generalization in multi-turn jailbreak detection. First, domain exposure via DAPT is the primary mechanism, bridging $\sim$60\,pp of the OOD gap on DeBERTa-v3 without any labels. Second, encoder pretraining method determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical: RoBERTa tolerates structurally simple attacks but fails on multi-persona ActorAttack (F1 0.981 $\to$ 0.466). Third, classification supervision functions as a representational regularizer, providing cross-seed stability (variance reduced 4--20$\times$ vs.\ MLM-only) and interpretable strategy characterization regardless of label correctness, though strategy predictions do not transfer to unseen attacks ($\kappa$=0.126). DAPT's detection advantage persists under adversarial paraphrasing (McNemar $p < 0.001$), though absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent. Validation on larger and more diverse datasets is needed.
19	
20	\section{Limitations}
21	\label{sec:limitations}
22	
23	\paragraph{Dataset scale and diversity.}
24	The training set contains 500 synthetic conversations generated by a single model (Qwen3-8B), and OOD evaluation covers three attack families (FITD, Deceptive Delight, ActorAttack). This scale limits statistical power: the perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) means tier boundaries in Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} correspond to 1--3 misclassifications. Evaluation on ToxicChat (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}) confirms that the domain-adapted vs.\ vanilla gap transfers to human-written jailbreaks, partially addressing the single-LLM confound. However, ToxicChat contains only single-turn data; whether the full multi-turn evidence hierarchy (including classification supervision's stabilization benefit) generalizes to human-generated multi-turn attacks remains open.
25	
26	\paragraph{Lexical separability.}
27	TF-IDF logistic regression achieves F1 = 0.961 on OOD Deceptive Delight with near-perfect per-prefix performance, indicating substantial lexical separability on this attack family. On DD alone, we cannot distinguish whether continued pretraining captures discourse features or exploits the same lexical cues. ActorAttack resolves this question: TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 while domain-adapted models maintain F1 $\geq$ 0.89, a $\geq$63\,pp gap, confirming that continued pretraining captures features beyond lexical overlap on structurally complex attacks. Whether this discourse-level advantage extends to other structurally complex attack families beyond ActorAttack requires further evaluation.
28	
29	\paragraph{Strategy annotation quality.}
30	Persuasion strategy labels are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting. Inter-annotator agreement with a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\kappa$=0.78, which indicates substantial but imperfect agreement. The small human validation sample and reliance on a single annotator model limit confidence in label quality. We plan to release the annotation prompts and data to enable independent verification.
31	
32	\paragraph{OOD strategy prediction.}
33	While the 9-class model achieves high detection F1 on OOD attacks (0.997$\pm$0.005 on Deceptive Delight), its strategy-level predictions do not generalize: OOD strategy accuracy is 30.7\% ($\kappa$=0.126). The interpretability provided by the persuasion taxonomy is currently limited to known attack families where strategy labels are reliable.
34	
35	\paragraph{Single-seed variants.}
36	Mean pooling results report a single-seed (seed 42) evaluation. Multi-seed validation is needed to confirm this finding.
37	
38	\paragraph{Scope.}
39	Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope. Single-turn token-level attacks~\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive multi-turn strategies, and attacks targeting modalities other than text are not addressed. The user-only input assumption (excluding assistant responses) may miss information in attacks where the model's own responses contribute to the escalation trajectory.
40	
41	\paragraph{Computation cost.}
42	Stage~1 continued pretraining requires approximately 30 minutes on a single A100 GPU per variant. Stage~2 training completes in under 5 minutes. Total pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours, making the approach accessible for replication.
43	
```

</details>
